379 — Shamir Secret Sharing + Threshold Signatures from Scratch

Project Number: 379

Project Name: Shamir Secret Sharing + Threshold Signatures from Scratch

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Status: specification — locked

1. Goal

1.1 What Is Built

A from-scratch threshold cryptography library in Rust: Shamir secret sharing (a t-of-n split via a random degree-(t-1) polynomial over a prime field, reconstructed by Lagrange interpolation) hardened into verifiable secret sharing (Feldman and Pedersen commitments), a dealerless distributed key generation protocol, and threshold signatures (FROST threshold Schnorr and threshold BLS) where any t of n parties jointly sign under a single public key while fewer than t learn nothing and cannot forge.

System boundaries:

Layer In scope Out of scope
Field arithmetic Constant-time F_p add/sub/mul/inv General-purpose multi-precision integers
Group arithmetic EC scalar mul, point add, hash-to-point Curve parameter generation, general ECC
Secret sharing Shamir split/reconstruct, additive reshare Secret backup storage, transport encoding
VSS Feldman, Pedersen commitments ZK proofs beyond VSS commitment verification
DKG Pedersen-based dealerless DKG, complaint round Asynchronous Byzantine DKG
Threshold Schnorr FROST per RFC 9591 Non-threshold Schnorr schemes
Threshold BLS Partial sigs, Lagrange aggregation BLS multisig, BLS batch verify
Proactive refresh Share re-randomization, lost-share recovery Threshold decryption, PVSS
Orchestration Typed round messages, transcript, identifiable abort Real network transport, TLS
CLI split/reconstruct/dkg/sign/verify/refresh GUI, web UI, REST API

Non-goals: general MPC/garbled circuits, oblivious RAM, ECDH key exchange, certificate management, production KMS integration, asynchronous networking, hardware security modules, post-quantum threshold schemes (no finalized standard as of this writing).

1.2 Intended Deployment Model

  • Library crate: embedded in applications requiring threshold key custody, distributed signing, or secret backup without a trusted party.
  • CLI binary (tss-cli): simulated multi-party protocols on a single host; integration testing; operator tooling.
  • Target environment: Linux/macOS, single-machine for development; multi-process simulation via CLI; production deployment requires a caller-supplied authenticated channel.

1.3 Target Users / Operators / Developers

Persona Use
Security engineers Embed threshold signing in wallet, CA, or HSM-replacement flows
Protocol researchers Study and extend FROST, BLS, DKG implementations
Systems learners Understand finite-field arithmetic, VSS, and round-based protocols from source
Auditors Audit constant-time paths, commitment verification, zeroization

1.4 Phase 0 Deliverable — Locked Crates

The following crates constitute the Phase 0 deliverable. All must compile (cargo check) before any threshold protocol is written.

  1. tss-core — Scalar, Share, ShareId, ThresholdParams, error types, secure CSPRNG wrappers; no I/O.
  2. tss-field — Prime-field arithmetic: constant-time add/sub/mul/inv (Fermat or extended-GCD), field element sampling.
  3. tss-shamir — split(secret, t, n), reconstruct(shares), additive reshare.
  4. tss-group — EC group: scalar mul (Montgomery ladder or fixed-window), point add, generators g/h, hash-to-scalar, hash-to-point.
  5. tss-vss — Feldman and Pedersen VSS: commitment generation, share verification.
  6. tss-dkg — Dealerless DKG: per-party VSS dealing, contribution aggregation, complaint round, joint public key derivation.
  7. tss-schnorr — FROST round 1 and round 2: nonce commitments, binding factors, partial sign, aggregate.
  8. tss-bls — Partial BLS signatures, Lagrange aggregation, pairing-based verify (pairing optionally deferred).
  9. tss-proactive — Proactive share refresh, lost-share recovery.
  10. tss-protocol — Typed round messages, transcript, identifiable-abort handling, round orchestration.
  11. tss-cli — split | reconstruct | dkg | sign | verify | refresh subcommands wiring simulated participants.

1.5 What Later Phases Unlock

Gate Unlocked capability
Phase 1–3 done Basic Shamir split/reconstruct; pedagogically self-contained
Phase 4–5 done Resharing, group arithmetic; prerequisite for all commitment-based protocols
Phase 6–7 done Share verification; DKG possible
Phase 8 done Full dealerless key generation; no trusted party required
Phase 9–10 done FROST threshold Schnorr signing (RFC 9591 conformance)
Phase 11 done Threshold BLS (pairing dependency resolved)
Phase 12 done Proactive security; epochs limit attacker's window
Phase 13 done End-to-end CLI simulation; identifiable abort; protocol transcripts
Phase 14 done Fuzz-hardened, constant-time audited, packed binary

1.6 Deferred Features

Feature Gate

Asynchronous Byzantine DKG PhaseDeferred("Phase 8")
Threshold decryption (El Gamal / ECIES) PhaseDeferred("Alternative Architectures — Alt-3")
Real network transport (TCP/QUIC) PhaseDeferred("Phase 13")

1.7 Timeline

Horizon Scope
Phase 0 (weeks 1–2) Workspace scaffold, tss-core, tss-field compiling
Phase 1–4 (weeks 2–6) Full Shamir: split, reconstruct, reshare, edge cases; property tests pass
Phase 5–7 (weeks 6–10) Group arithmetic, Feldman VSS, Pedersen VSS
Phase 8 (weeks 10–14) DKG end-to-end
Phase 9–10 (weeks 14–20) FROST round 1 + round 2; RFC 9591 test vectors pass
Phase 11 (weeks 20–24) Threshold BLS
Phase 12–13 (weeks 24–30) Proactive refresh, protocol orchestration, CLI
Phase 14 (weeks 30–36+) Hardening, conformance, fuzz, constant-time audit

Estimated total: 30–40 weeks for a complete, fuzz-hardened, RFC-conformant implementation.

1.8 Language Rationale

Rust is mandatory. Threshold cryptography is finite-field and elliptic-curve arithmetic with strict constant-time secret handling: Lagrange interpolation over a prime field, scalar multiplication for commitments and signatures, and multi-round protocols whose security collapses if a secret share or nonce leaks via timing. Rust gives field/group abstractions, constant-time scalar operations, exhaustive enums for the protocol round/message taxonomy, and guaranteed secret zeroization. The field, Shamir core, VSS, DKG, threshold Schnorr/FROST, and threshold BLS layers are all Rust; the pairing for BLS verification is a documented, optionally-deferred dependency.

1.9 Required Prerequisites

Domain Specific requirement
Abstract algebra Prime fields F_p: closure, inverses, characteristic; polynomial rings over fields
Number theory Fermat's little theorem (a^{p-1} ≡ 1 mod p), extended Euclidean algorithm
Elliptic curves Weierstrass / Edwards / Montgomery forms; group law; discrete-log hardness
Lagrange interpolation Barycentric form; evaluation at x=0; uniqueness over a field
Commitment schemes Hiding vs. binding; Pedersen commitments; homomorphic properties
Schnorr signatures nonce-challenge-response; discrete-log binding; nonce reuse catastrophe
Pairings (BLS) Bilinear maps e(aP, Q) = e(P, aQ); pairing-based verification equation
Rust Traits, const generics, zeroize crate, subtle crate for constant-time ops
Protocol analysis Round complexity; broadcast vs. authenticated channels; Byzantine adversary model

2. Why Build This

2.1 Unique Systems Knowledge

Topic What is learned
Finite-field arithmetic from scratch Constant-time modular inversion; Montgomery reduction trade-offs; why branch-on-secret is catastrophic
Lagrange interpolation in cryptography Exact arithmetic over F_p vs. floating point; barycentric form; why the constant term is the secret
VSS commitment binding Why Feldman leaks g^secret; why Pedersen adds a blinding polynomial; soundness vs. hiding distinction
Dealerless DKG How parties can jointly establish a key no one holds; complaint round mechanics; qualified set construction
FROST nonce binding Why a single rogue nonce attack breaks naive threshold Schnorr; how binding factors prevent it; Drijvers et al.
Proactive security Epoch-based share refresh; zero-sum reshare; why the attacker window is bounded to one epoch
Identifiable abort How a protocol can finger a cheating participant; publicly verifiable evidence; accountability vs. robustness
Pairing-based crypto Bilinear map verification; why BLS aggregation is linear; why the pairing is a one-way trapdoor

2.2 Historical and Architectural Significance

  • Shamir (1979): the foundational result — a secret is split information-theoretically; t-1 shares are worthless.
  • Feldman (1987) + Pedersen (1991): made VSS practical and removed the requirement to trust the dealer's honesty during reconstruction.
  • GJKR DKG (1999/2007): removed the trusted dealer entirely; all modern threshold systems descend from this or a simplification of it.
  • FROST (2020) + RFC 9591 (2024): the first efficient, provably-secure two-round threshold Schnorr; deployed in Zcash, Cloudflare, and DFINITY.
  • Threshold BLS (Boldyreva 2003): aggregation-in-the-exponent via Lagrange; pairing verification; basis for Drand, BLS wallet key custody, and Ethereum distributed validator technology.
  • Proactive security (Herzberg 1995): extends the secrecy window to the intersection of epochs; necessary for long-lived keys.

2.3 Engineering Muscles Developed

  • Constant-time discipline: writing and auditing field arithmetic where branching on secret bits breaks security.
  • Protocol state-machine design: typed enums for DkgRound, round-locked message types, transcript binding.
  • Invariant-first testing: property-based tests for field axioms, interpolation correctness, VSS soundness before writing the protocol.
  • Dependency surface minimization: crypto-specific crates (subtle, zeroize, rand_core) vs. no application-level crates.
  • Multi-party simulation: simulating n parties in a single process; detecting protocol failures by injecting malicious messages.
  • Conformance testing: driving implementation against RFC 9591 fixed test vectors; fail-hard on any deviation.

2.4 Production Engineering Concepts Exercised

Concept Instantiation
Defense in depth Commitment verification at every receive; identifiable abort before any aggregation
Fail-closed error handling Under-threshold inputs return Err, never a wrong secret
Zeroization zeroize crate on Polynomial.coeffs, Share.value, nonce scalars on drop
Auditability Transcript records every public value exchanged; replay-verifiable
Epoch-bounded exposure Proactive refresh limits adversary to t-of-n within a single epoch
Conformance RFC 9591 test vector suite; FROST interop with ZcashFoundation/frost

2.5 Cross-Links to Portfolio Projects

Project Link
#90 — Curve25519/Ed25519 tss-group reuses or mirrors the group implementation; Schnorr base construction
#89 — RSA Contrast: RSA secret sharing is additive over Z, Shamir is polynomial over F_p
#92 — zk-SNARK Shared primitives: field arithmetic, elliptic curves, Pedersen commitments
#94 — PBFT Byzantine fault tolerance sibling; DKG complaint round is a Byzantine protocol
#400 — Signal/Double Ratchet Applied threshold crypto: key custody for Signal-style key trees

2.6 Calibration Value

Completing this project establishes: fluency in finite-field and EC arithmetic; understanding of the VSS/DKG/FROST security proof structure; ability to read and implement IETF cryptographic standards from RFCs; constant-time discipline applicable to any secret-handling path; and multi-party protocol engineering skills (round structure, complaint handling, transcript design) that transfer directly to MPC, distributed validators, and hardware security products.

2.7 What Mastery Looks Like

  • Phases 1–3: You can derive the Lagrange interpolation formula from first principles over F_p, explain why t-1 shares reveal nothing (cite Shannon entropy argument from B2/P1), implement constant-time field inversion, and catch timing-oracle bugs in code review by identifying if secret == 0 branches.
  • Phases 4–5: You can design a re-sharing protocol that changes (t, n) without reconstructing the secret, specify the exact sub-share verification round needed, and audit Montgomery-ladder scalar multiplication for data-independent control flow (P4/I9).
  • Phases 6–7: You can explain the Feldman/Pedersen security distinction (binding vs. binding+hiding), state exactly which algebraic assumption each relies on, and identify the generator independence requirement for Pedersen's hiding property (§P2, §5 P3).
  • Phase 8: You can trace the GJKR DKG bias problem (P4 paper note), explain why the complaint round must run even with zero complaints, and construct a qualified-set counterexample that breaks signing if the set check is omitted (§8, I6).
  • Phases 9–10: You can reproduce FROST's binding-factor derivation from RFC 9591 §4.3, explain why the Drijvers ROS attack fails against it (§P5/P6), and identify a missing-signer attack if the commitment list encoding is non-deterministic.
  • Phase 11: You can state the BLS12-381 pairing equation, explain why cofactor clearing on hash-to-G1 is mandatory, and reason about the small-subgroup attack if the check is omitted (§11.6, P8).
  • Phases 12–14: You can design a proactive refresh ceremony checklist, explain why a non-zero constant term in a refresh polynomial breaks the secret-invariance invariant (I8/P7), and write a dudect CI gate for any new function that touches a secret Scalar.

3. Architectural Overview

3.1 Canonical Architecture Diagram

                      SHAMIR SECRET SHARING + THRESHOLD SIGNATURES
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|   tss-cli (split / reconstruct / dkg / sign / verify / refresh)                      |
|        |                                                                            |
|        v                                                                            |
|   tss-shamir (split: random degree t-1 poly; reconstruct: Lagrange @ x=0)            |
|        |                                   ^                                        |
|        v                                   |  verify shares                         |
|   tss-vss (Feldman g^coeff / Pedersen hiding commitments)                            |
|        |                                                                            |
|        v                                                                            |
|   tss-dkg (each party VSS-deals; aggregate contributions -> JOINT PUBKEY;            |
|            complaint round disqualifies cheaters; no trusted dealer)                 |
|        |                          |                                                 |
|        v                          v                                                 |
|   tss-schnorr / FROST        tss-bls                                                 |
|   (round1: nonce commits;    (partial sigma_i = H(m)^{share};                        |
|    round2: partial z_i;       aggregate via Lagrange;                                |
|    aggregate (R,z))           pairing verify)                                        |
|        |                          |                                                 |
|        +------------+-------------+                                                 |
|                     v                                                               |
|   tss-proactive (refresh shares; recover lost shares; secret unchanged)              |
|                     |                                                               |
|                     v                                                               |
|   tss-field (constant-time F_p)  +  tss-group (EC scalar mul / points)               |
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

3.2 Subsystem Descriptions

tss-cli. Entry point for all human interaction and integration testing. Parses subcommands, constructs ThresholdParams, instantiates simulated participant objects, drives protocol rounds in sequence, serializes/deserializes shares and signatures. No cryptographic logic; delegates entirely to library crates. Trust boundary: untrusted CLI input; all inputs are validated by library types before use. No persistent state between runs unless the caller supplies key files.

tss-shamir. Implements split (sample a random degree-(t-1) polynomial with coeffs[0] = secret, evaluate at n distinct non-zero ShareIds via Horner's method) and reconstruct (Lagrange interpolation at x=0 over any t distinct shares). The dealer's polynomial and all intermediate evaluations are zeroized after use. Data flow in: (secret: Scalar, params: ThresholdParams, rng); data flow out: Vec<Share>. Reconstruction in: Vec<Share> (length ≥ t); out: Scalar. No I/O; no EC operations. Trust boundary: the caller is responsible for secure share distribution; this crate is purely algebraic.

tss-vss. Lifts the plain Shamir sharing into the group to enable public verifiability. Feldman: dealer publishes Commitment = Vec<Point> where C_j = g^{a_j}; recipient verifies g^{s_i} == product C_j^{i^j}. Pedersen: dealer publishes PedersenCommitment = Vec<Point> where C_j = g^{a_j} h^{b_j} using a secondary blinding polynomial; recipient verifies with both sub-shares. Data flow in (verify): (Share, Commitment, params); out: Result<(), VssError>. Dependency: tss-group for EC ops, tss-field for exponent arithmetic. Feldman is binding but not hiding (g^secret is exposed in C_0); Pedersen is both.

tss-dkg. Each of n parties acts as a Pedersen VSS dealer and broadcasts their commitments and encrypted sub-shares. Each party verifies received sub-shares, broadcasts complaints for invalid ones; the qualified set is the set of dealers whose shares passed all verifications. Each party's final KeyShare.secret_share = sum of received sub-shares from qualified dealers. KeyShare.group_public = sum of C_0 commitments from qualified dealers. No party ever holds or computes the joint secret scalar. State machine transitions: DkgRound::Commit → Share → Complaint → Finalize. Trust boundary: authenticated but potentially Byzantine peers; complaint round provides identifiable abort.

tss-schnorr / FROST. Two-round threshold Schnorr per RFC 9591. Round 1: each signer samples hiding nonce d_i, binding nonce e_i, publishes NonceCommitment = (D_i = g^{d_i}, E_i = g^{e_i}). Binding factor: rho_i = H(i, msg, commitment-list). Group nonce: R = sum(D_i + rho_i * E_i). Round 2: each signer computes PartialSignature z_i = d_i + rho_i*e_i + lambda_i*share_i*c where c = H(R, Y, msg) and lambda_i is its Lagrange coefficient over the signing set. Aggregate: z = sum(z_i). Verify: g^z == R * Y^c. Partial-sig verification enables identifiable abort before aggregation. Nonces d_i, e_i are zeroized immediately after use.

tss-bls. Each signer computes PartialSignature sigma_i = H(m)^{share_i} (hash-to-curve, then scalar mul with the share scalar). Aggregation: sigma = prod(sigma_i^{lambda_i}) where lambda_i is the Lagrange coefficient for signer i over the signing set — equivalent to H(m)^{secret} by linearity of the exponent. Verify: e(sigma, g) == e(H(m), Y) via bilinear pairing. The pairing backend is a declared dependency (e.g., bls12_381 crate); if unavailable, the verify step is gated behind PhaseDeferred("Phase 11"). Partial signature verification: e(sigma_i, g) == e(H(m), Y_i) where Y_i = g^{share_i} from the DKG public share.

tss-proactive. Periodic refresh: each party i samples a fresh degree-(t-1) polynomial r_i with r_i(0) = 0 (zero constant term), VSS-deals it, and sends sub-shares to all parties. Each party j's refreshed share = old_share_j + sum(r_i(j)). The joint secret is unchanged (sum of zero-constant-term polynomials adds zero). Lost-share recovery: a party with no share requests Lagrange interpolation assistance from t other parties; those parties each compute and send lambda_i * share_i (blinded by the refresh); recipient reconstructs. All intermediate values are zeroized. Epoch boundary: refresh must complete before the next epoch begins; partial refresh is not accepted.

tss-field + tss-group. Foundation layer. tss-field: constant-time F_p with explicit rejection of zero-inverse, prime = curve scalar order. All operations use the subtle crate for conditional assignment; no secret-dependent branching. tss-group: wraps a prime-order EC group (reusing portfolio #90 curve25519-dalek or equivalent); exposes scalar_mul(Point, Scalar) → Point, point_add, generator() → Point, alt_generator() → Point (independent h for Pedersen), hash_to_scalar, hash_to_point. Neither crate performs I/O or protocol logic.

tss-protocol. Typed message envelopes: each round message carries sender ShareId, round tag, and serialized payload. Transcript: a running hash of all public values broadcast (commitments, nonces, partial sigs) in canonical order; used to bind the protocol session and prevent replay. Identifiable abort: if a partial signature or VSS share fails verification, the transcript + evidence is retained and the bad actor's ShareId is recorded. Round orchestration: drives a Vec<Box<dyn ParticipantState>> through rounds, collecting messages and dispatching them, without performing any cryptographic operations itself.

4. System Constraints

4.1 Quantified Engineering Targets

Constraint Target Rationale
Field element size 32 bytes (256-bit scalar) Curve scalar field for Ed25519/Ristretto255 or secp256k1
Point size (compressed) 32–33 bytes Ed25519/Ristretto: 32; secp256k1: 33
Max n (participants) 255 ShareId(u32) but practical DKG limits complexity; 255 is the standard ceiling
Max t (threshold) n Must satisfy 1 ≤ t ≤ n; checked at ThresholdParams construction
Polynomial memory (t) × 32 bytes degree-(t-1); t=100 → 3.2 KB; zeroized after use
Share vector memory n × 64 bytes n=255 → ~16 KB; Share = {ShareId(4) + Scalar(32)} plus padding
Commitment vector memory t × 32 bytes (Feldman) t=100 → 3.2 KB of EC points
Pedersen commitment memory t × 64 bytes two generators × t coefficients
DKG per-party message size n × (64 + 64) bytes ≈ 32 KB at n=255 sub-share + Pedersen commitment per peer
DKG total message complexity O(n²) Each of n parties sends to each of n parties
Shamir split latency (p50) < 1 ms (t=10, n=20) Field mul is ~10 ns; Horner evaluation is t muls per share
Shamir reconstruct latency (p50) < 1 ms (t=10) t Lagrange coefficient computations + t field muls
Feldman VSS commitment (p50) < 5 ms (t=10) t scalar muls (EC); each ~100–200 µs on a modern core
Feldman VSS verify (p50) < 5 ms (t=10) t+1 scalar muls + t point adds per recipient
DKG full round-trip (n=10, t=6) < 500 ms simulated Dominated by n² scalar muls for commitments
FROST round 1 per party < 10 ms 2 scalar muls (nonce commitments) + hash
FROST round 2 per party < 10 ms 2 field muls + Lagrange coefficient + scalar mul
FROST aggregate (t=10) < 5 ms t field additions
FROST verify < 2 ms 2 scalar muls + 1 point add + 1 hash
BLS partial sig per party < 20 ms hash-to-curve + scalar mul
BLS aggregate (t=10) < 10 ms t Lagrange scalar muls + t point adds
BLS pairing verify < 30 ms two pairings on bls12_381
Proactive refresh (n=10, t=6) < 1 s n DKG-like rounds of zero-sum sharing
Stack depth < 64 frames per protocol call No unbounded recursion
Heap allocation per sign < 1 MB Vec allocations for commitment lists and partial sigs
Secret zeroization latency < 1 µs zeroize crate; volatile writes to prevent optimizer elision
CSPRNG source OS entropy (getrandom) No user-supplied PRNGs for key material
Constant-time paths All share/nonce/field-inverse operations Verified by timing tests + audit; subtle crate enforced
Thread safety No shared mutable state in library crates Protocol state per participant; no global mutables
Fuzz corpus size ≥ 10,000 seeds per fuzz target Covering malformed shares, commitments, partial sigs
Test coverage (unit + property) > 90% line coverage on tss-field, tss-shamir, tss-vss cargo-llvm-cov enforced in CI
RFC 9591 test vector pass rate 100% All FROST published vectors must match exactly
Binary size (tss-cli, stripped) < 10 MB No unnecessary dependencies; LTO enabled
Compile time (clean, debug) < 120 s on 8-core 3 GHz Workspace-level; parallel crate compilation
Minimum supported Rust version Stable, MSRV pinned in workspace No nightly-only features in library crates

4.2 Failure Budget

Failure class Allowed rate Detection
Reconstruction of wrong secret Zero (hard invariant I1) Property tests + conformance suite
Share verification false positive Zero (hard invariant I5) Adversarial fuzz inputs
Timing oracle on secret share Zero (hard invariant I9) Constant-time audit + timing CI
DKG joint pubkey mismatch Zero (hard invariant I6) Round-trip test: sign with threshold sig, verify under joint key
Proactive refresh changes secret Zero (hard invariant I8) Before/after reconstruct test per epoch
Panic in library crates on bad input Zero #[no_panic] annotation on hot paths; fuzz must not panic
Memory leak of secret material Zero Valgrind / address-sanitizer in CI; zeroize drop impls

4.3 Deployment and Scaling Assumptions

Assumption Value
Network model Authenticated point-to-point channels (caller-supplied); this library provides message types only
Synchrony assumption Synchronous rounds with caller-supplied timeouts; no async runtime in library crates
Adversary model Static, rushing, Byzantine minority (fewer than t corrupt parties)
Key lifetime One epoch per proactive refresh cycle; epoch duration is caller-configurable
Max concurrent DKG sessions One per process in the reference CLI; library crates are stateless and re-entrant
Storage per key share ~100 bytes (KeyShare serialized)
Audit log retention Transcript retained in memory for the duration of the signing session; caller serializes to disk if needed

5. Required Reading

Books

# Title / Author / Edition Chapters to Study Concepts to Extract Applied In (§/Phase)
B1 Handbook of Applied Cryptography — Menezes, van Oorschot, Vanstone (1996, free PDF) Ch. 3 sec. 3.5 (number theory); Ch. 11 (digital signatures); Ch. 12 sec. 12.7 (secret sharing) Lagrange interpolation over F_p; Blakley vs. Shamir; polynomial evaluation; modular inverse via ext-gcd §P1, §8 (§8.6 Lagrange derivation), Phase 1 (field arithmetic), Phase 3 (reconstruct); Comparative Architecture Table (Blakley entry)
B2 Introduction to Modern Cryptography — Katz & Lindell, 3rd ed. Ch. 3 sec. 3.1–3.3 (information-theoretic security); Ch. 12 sec. 12.1–12.3 (threshold protocols) Perfect secrecy formal definition; conditional entropy; threshold secret sharing; UC security framework §I2 (perfect-secrecy proof), §P1 (information-theoretic bound), Phase 2–3 (correctness argument), §7.2 (What Mastery)
B3 A Course in Number Theory and Cryptography — Koblitz, 2nd ed. Ch. 4 sec. 4.1–4.3 (finite fields); Ch. 6 sec. 6.1–6.3 (elliptic curves over F_p) F_p arithmetic (add/sub/mul); Fermat's little theorem for inverse; EC group law; point at infinity §P4, Phase 1 (tss-field: Fermat inverse), Phase 5 (tss-group EC group law), §4.1 System Constraints (field-element size)
B4 Cryptography: Theory and Practice — Stinson & Paterson, 4th ed. Ch. 7 sec. 7.1–7.4 (secret sharing schemes); Ch. 9 sec. 9.3–9.5 (Schnorr signatures) Information-theoretic bound; access structures; ramp schemes; Schnorr unforgeability proof §I1–I2, §P1 (formal secrecy bound), Phase 9–10 (Schnorr unforgeability argument for FROST partial-sig security)
B5 Guide to Elliptic Curve Cryptography — Hankerson, Menezes, Vanstone Ch. 2 (finite field arithmetic); Ch. 3 (EC arithmetic — NAF, window methods, Montgomery); Ch. 4 sec. 4.4 (parameter validation) Montgomery ladder; w-NAF scalar mul; constant-time selection; cofactor; twist attacks; subgroup check §P4, §I9, Phase 5 (tss-group scalar mul), §T5 threat model (off-curve point), Phase 14 (CT audit)
B6 Pairings for Beginners — Craig Costello (free PDF, 2012) Ch. 1–2 (bilinear maps, Weil/Tate pairings); Ch. 3 (Miller loop); Ch. 5–6 (BLS signatures; pairing-friendly curves) Bilinearity: e(aP, bQ)=e(P,Q)^{ab}; Miller loop algorithm; BLS12-381 tower; signature aggregation via pairing §P8 (verify equation), Phase 11 (tss-bls pairing verify, hash-to-G1), §8.5 (BLS aggregate sequence), Comparative Architecture Table (This design / BLS12-381 column)
B7 Real-World Cryptography — Wong Ch. 8 (randomness and entropy); Ch. 11 (protocols); Ch. 12 (threshold and multi-party crypto) CSPRNG construction; entropy depletion pitfalls; nonce hygiene; key lifecycle; zeroization in hardware and software §P9 (zeroize), §ADR-12 (RNG choice), Phase 2 (CSPRNG sampling), Phase 9 (nonce hygiene), §F10 failure mode (CSPRNG failure)
B8 The Mathematics of Secrets — Holden Ch. 6–7 (finite fields and secret sharing) Accessible treatment of polynomial interpolation; Galois field extensions vs. prime fields Phase 3 secondary reference for Lagrange formula verification; §8.6 worked example (pedagogical cross-check); §2.7 What Mastery (communicating scheme to non-experts)
